 \qquad
 \alpha((g,h),(g',h'))=\mu(g,g')\overline{\omega(h,h')}.$$ On an $M$–$N$ correspondence the joint group action is <a id="eq:calculus-joint-action"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-joint-action}
 U_{(g,h)}\xi=u_g\xi v_h^*.$$ It has multiplier $\alpha$. Indeed, its rightmost product is $v_{h'}^*v_h^*=(v_hv_{h'})^*$, so $U_{(g,h)}U_{(g',h')}=
\mu(g,g')\overline{\omega(h,h')}U_{(gg',hh')}$. In particular the two coordinate actions commute exactly.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Germs and their full stabilizers

A germ from $\Gamma$ to $\Lambda$ is an isomorphism $\delta:A\to B$ between finite-index subgroups, modulo agreement after restriction to a common finite-index subgroup. Composition means composition on a finite-index domain where both representatives are defined. For example, if $\varepsilon:C\to F$ is a representative from $\Lambda$ to a third group, then $\varepsilon\delta$ is defined on $\delta^{-1}(B\cap C)$ and has image $\varepsilon(B\cap C)$. Both have finite index in the relevant endpoint group. Restricting representatives further does not change the germ, and a common further restriction verifies associativity. Inversion is represented by the inverse subgroup isomorphism.

For a germ $d$ from $\Gamma$ to $\Lambda$, let $$(g,h)d=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)\,d\,\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g^{-1}),\qquad
 C_d=\{(g,h)\in D:(g,h)d=d\}.$$ If $\delta:A\to B$ represents $d$, denote its graph by $C_\delta=\{(a,\delta(a)):a\in A\}$. We distinguish this graph from the full stabilizer $C_d$.

<a id="stmt-lem-calculus-germ-test"></a>
<div id="lem:calculus-germ-test" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Full stabilizers and the finite-orbit test). *The maps $g\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}[\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g)]$ and $h\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}[\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)]$ are injective. The group $C_d$ is the graph of an isomorphism $\delta_d:A_d\to B_d$ between finite-index subgroups. It contains $C_\delta$ at finite index, and $\delta_d$ extends $\delta$. If a finite-index subgroup of $C_d$ fixes another germ $x$ from $\Gamma$ to $\Lambda$, then $x=d$. Equivalently, the only finite $C_d$-orbit in the space of these germs is $\{d\}$. The same conclusion holds with $C_d$ replaced by $C_\delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-calculus-germ-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-calculus-germ-test</summary>

*Proof.* The centralizer of a finite-index subgroup of an ICC group is trivial: an element of that centralizer has a centralizer of finite index and therefore a finite conjugacy class. This proves injectivity of the inner-germ maps.

<a id="eq:calculus-stabilizer-relation"></a>
The stabilizer condition can be written $$\label{eq:calculus-stabilizer-relation}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)=d\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g)d^{-1}.$$ If either coordinate of an element of $C_d$ is the identity, injectivity of the inner-germ maps forces the other coordinate to be the identity. Thus both coordinate projections on $C_d$ are injective. Their images $A_d$ and $B_d$ form the domain and range of a group isomorphism $\delta_d$. The inclusion $C_\delta\subset C_d$ follows by evaluating Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-stabilizer-relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-stabilizer-relation">[eq:calculus-stabilizer-relation]</a> on a common finite-index domain. Consequently $A\subset A_d$, $B\subset B_d$, and $\delta_d|_A=\delta$. Both projected groups have finite index, and $[C_d:C_\delta]=[A_d:A]<\infty$.

<a id="eq:calculus-finite-orbit-test"></a>
Suppose $C_0\leq C_d$ has finite index and fixes $x$. Its first projection $A_0$ has finite index in $\Gamma$. For every $(a,b)\in C_0$ the two stabilizer equations give $$\label{eq:calculus-finite-orbit-test}
 x\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(a)x^{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(b)=d\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(a)d^{-1}.$$ Choose representatives $\chi$ and $\delta$ for $x$ and $d$, and restrict $a$ to $A_0\cap\operatorname{dom}(\chi)\cap
\operatorname{dom}(\delta)$. On this finite-index subgroup the outer expressions in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-finite-orbit-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-finite-orbit-test">[eq:calculus-finite-orbit-test]</a> are the inner germs of $\chi(a)$ and $\delta(a)$. Injectivity in $\Lambda$ gives $\chi(a)=\delta(a)$, whence $x=d$. A finite orbit has a finite-index point stabilizer, so this proves the equivalent orbit statement. Finally, a finite-index subgroup of $C_\delta$ also has finite index in $C_d$. ◻

</details>

The finite-orbit conclusion will make germ labels intrinsic to the induced models. Cosets of $C_d$ correspond to actual germs, whereas several cosets of a presenting graph may represent the same germ. After grouping those cosets, a bounded intertwiner will have matrix blocks between the resulting finite-dimensional fibres. Equivariance makes a block’s norm constant along a stabilizer orbit. The lemma will therefore force such a map to preserve each germ label, once square summability of its columns is used.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Projective induction and the arithmetic model

Projective induction, induction in stages, and double-coset tensor decompositions belong to Mackey’s representation-theoretic framework (Mackey 1958, Theorems 4.1, 4.2, and 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Mackey1958](#ref-Mackey1958)]. The following coordinates fix the multiplier throughout and prove every scalar identity used here.

<a id="eq:input-A1"></a>
Let $C\leq D$ be a subgroup and let $\gamma$ be a normalized unitary projective representation on a finite-dimensional space $V$, with multiplier $\alpha|_{C\times C}$. Write $X=D/C$ for left cosets. Choose representatives $r_x\in D$, with $r_C=e$, and give each $x\in X$ a copy $V_x$ of $V$. Set $\mathcal H(C,\gamma)=\bigoplus_{x\in X}V_x$, with the counting Hilbert norm. For $s\in D$ put $$\label{eq:input-A1}
 \begin{split}
 c(s,x)&=r_{sx}^{-1}sr_x,\\
 U^X_s(x)&=
 \frac{\alpha(s,r_x)}{\alpha(r_{sx},c(s,x))}\,
 \gamma(c(s,x)):V_x\longrightarrow V_{sx}.
 \end{split}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-calculus-induction"></a>
<div id="lem:calculus-induction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Exact projective induction). *Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a> defines a unitary projective representation of $D$ with multiplier $\alpha$. Changing the coset representatives gives a unitarily equivalent bundle by unitary identifications of its individual fibres. If $C$ is the graph of a finite-index subgroup isomorphism $A\to B$, the bundle has commuting normal $M$ and right $N$ actions, and is bifinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-calculus-induction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-calculus-induction</summary>

<a id="eq:calculus-transport-identity"></a>
*Proof.* The correction $c(s,x)$ belongs to $C$, and $$c(s,tx)c(t,x)=c(st,x).$$ Using the cocycle identity for $\alpha$ and the projective multiplication of $\gamma$ in Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a> gives $$\label{eq:calculus-transport-identity}
 U^X_s(tx)U^X_t(x)=\alpha(s,t)U^X_{st}(x).$$ One can also check all the scalar factors at once in the central extension with multiplication $(z,s)(w,t)=(zw\alpha(s,t),st)$: the representation of its preimage of $C$ is $(z,c)\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}z\gamma(c)$, and the equality $sr_x=r_{sx}c(s,x)$ gives exactly the ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a>. Thus this argument retains, rather than discards, the fixed multiplier.

For explicit independence of representatives, suppose $r'_x=r_xk_x$, where $k_x\in C$. The identification from the new coordinate copy of $V_x$ to the old one is $$F_x=\alpha(r_x,k_x)^{-1}\gamma(k_x).$$ Indeed identifying a fibre by $U_{r'_x}$ instead of $U_{r_x}$ has precisely this effect. Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-transport-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-transport-identity">[eq:calculus-transport-identity]</a> then gives $F_{sx}U'{}^X_s(x)=U^X_s(x)F_x$.

Now assume that $C$ is a graph. Both coordinate groups act freely on $X$. For example, if $(g,e)r_xC=r_xC$, then $r_x^{-1}(g,e)r_x\in C$ has second coordinate $e$, so $g=e$. On an orbit of the first coordinate group choose a fibre $V_{x_0}$. The unitary identification $$\ell^2(\Gamma)\otimes V_{x_0}\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{g\in\Gamma}V_{(g,e)x_0},
 \qquad \delta_g\otimes\xi\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}U^X_{(g,e)}\xi$$ intertwines its action with $\lambda_\mu\otimes1$, since $U_{(a,e)}U_{(g,e)}=\mu(a,g)U_{(ag,e)}$. Hence it extends normally to $L_\mu(\Gamma)$. The same argument for the second coordinate gives copies of $\lambda_{\bar\omega}$, which are the standard normal representations of the generators acting by right adjoints $\xi\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\xi v_h^*$. The two actions commute, because the product cocycle has trivial mixed-coordinate factors.

The first coordinate orbits are indexed by $\Lambda/B$, and the second coordinate orbits by $\Gamma/A$. They are finite in number. Each is a regular module with multiplicity $\dim_{\mathbb C}V$, proving bifiniteness as well as the dimension formulas below. ◻

</details>

We next identify this construction with the Connes-product model in Theorem <a href="#thm:correspondences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:correspondences">4</a>. This also supplies a direct normality check of the subgroup action used to define that product.

<a id="stmt-lem-calculus-graph-model"></a>
<div id="lem:calculus-graph-model" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (The graph-bundle unitary). *Let $\delta:A\to B$ be a finite-index subgroup isomorphism and let $\sigma:A\to\mathcal U(V)$ have multiplier $\beta=\mu|_{A\times A}/\delta^*\omega$. Define $\gamma(a,\delta(a))=\sigma(a)$. Then $$E(A,B,\delta,\sigma)=
 L^2(M)\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_{L_{\mu|A}(A)}
       (V\otimes L^2(N))
 \cong\mathcal H(C_\delta,\gamma).$$ The unitary sends the vector $\xi$ in the fibre labelled by $x$ to $U_{r_x}j\xi$, where $j\xi=\widehat1\otimes(\xi\otimes\widehat1)$ in the displayed model.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-calculus-graph-model"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-calculus-graph-model</summary>

*Proof.* On $V\otimes L^2(N)$ the subgroup action is $$u_a(\xi\otimes\eta)=\sigma(a)\xi\otimes v_{\delta(a)}\eta.$$ Its multiplier is $\beta(a,b)\omega(\delta(a),\delta(b))=\mu(a,b)$. For normality, choose representatives $t$ for $B\backslash\Lambda$. On the summand spanned by $\xi\otimes v_{\delta(a)}v_t$, the map <a id="eq:calculus-absorption"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-absorption}
 \xi\otimes v_{\delta(a)}v_t
 \mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\delta_a\otimes\sigma(a)^*\xi
 \quad\text{in }\ell^2(A)\otimes V$$ is unitary and conjugates the subgroup action to $\lambda_{\mu|A}\otimes1$. To check the phase, applying $u_b$ before this map produces $$\omega(\delta(b),\delta(a))
 \sigma(ba)^*\sigma(b)
 =\omega(\delta(b),\delta(a))\beta(b,a)\sigma(a)^*
 =\mu(b,a)\sigma(a)^*.$$ Thus the left subgroup action is normal. Its right $N$ action is the standard action on the second factor and commutes with it.

<a id="eq:calculus-coset-model"></a>
Choose representatives $r$ for $\Gamma/A$. As a right $L_{\mu|A}(A)$ module, $L^2(M)$ has orthonormal free basis $u_r$; the assertion follows directly from $E_{L_{\mu|A}(A)}(u_r^*u_{r'})=\delta_{r,r'}1$. The relative product therefore identifies with the orthogonal sum $$\label{eq:calculus-coset-model}
 E(A,B,\delta,\sigma)
 =\bigoplus_{r\in\Gamma/A}u_r\otimes(V\otimes L^2(N)).$$ At its identity fibre the graph action is exactly $$U_{(a,\delta(a))}j\xi
 =u_a\otimes(\xi\otimes v_{\delta(a)}^*)
 =j\sigma(a)\xi.$$ For $g=ra$, balancing in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-coset-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-coset-model">[eq:calculus-coset-model]</a> gives the more explicit formula $$U_{(g,h)}j\xi
 =\mu(r,a)^{-1}u_r\otimes
       \bigl(\sigma(a)\xi\otimes v_{\delta(a)}v_h^*\bigr).$$ The last Fourier coordinate is $\delta(a)h^{-1}$, up to its displayed unit scalar. Thus two translated identity fibres are orthogonal unless their labels in $D/C_\delta$ agree: equality of their first cosets and last Fourier coordinates is exactly that coset equality. Conversely these fibres exhaust every summand in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-coset-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-coset-model">[eq:calculus-coset-model]</a>. They are an orthogonal, transitive system with graph action $\gamma$ at the basepoint. Finally $$U_sU_{r_x}j
 =\frac{\alpha(s,r_x)}{\alpha(r_{sx},c(s,x))}
       U_{r_{sx}}j\gamma(c(s,x)),$$ which proves that the stated unitary has exactly the transport of Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:calculus-regrouping"></a>
For a full stabilizer we write $$H(d,\gamma)=\mathcal H(C_d,\gamma).$$ Its labels identify with actual germs in the orbit $Dd$. There is also a canonical regrouping of an original graph model over this full-germ orbit. The map $D/C_\delta\to D/C_d$ has finite fibres. Its fibre above $C_d$ carries $$\label{eq:calculus-regrouping}
 \operatorname{Ind}^{C_d,\alpha}_{C_\delta}\gamma,$$ where projective induction uses Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a> with $D$ replaced by $C_d$ and with the restricted cocycle. Indeed that fibre is the sum indexed by $C_d/C_\delta$, and its transports are precisely that formula. Regrouping the orthogonal sum consequently gives $$\mathcal H(C_\delta,\gamma)
 \cong H\bigl(d,\operatorname{Ind}^{C_d,\alpha}_{C_\delta}\gamma\bigr).$$ This description includes the phases of finite induction; it does not replace the projective representation by an ordinary one.

Changing an orbit representative also retains a scalar transport. If $d'=kd$, then $C_{d'}=kC_dk^{-1}$. Identify the new base fibre with the old one using $U_k$. For $c'=kck^{-1}\in C_{d'}$ the representation in these coordinates is <a id="eq:calculus-representative-change"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-representative-change}
 \gamma'(c')=
 \frac{\alpha(c',k)}{\alpha(k,c)}\,\gamma(c).$$ Indeed $U_{c'}U_k=
\alpha(c',k)\alpha(k,c)^{-1}U_kU_c$. This gives the exact representative-change equivalence, including its restricted multiplier. Two choices of $k$ leading to the same germ give equivalent fibres by the stabilizer action itself.

<a id="stmt-prop-calculus-dimensions"></a>
<div id="prop:calculus-dimensions" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (Dimensions of the actual orbit modules). *For a finite-dimensional $\alpha|_{C_d}$-projective representation $\gamma$, <a id="eq:calculus-full-dimensions"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-full-dimensions}
 \dim_M H(d,\gamma)
   =\dim_{\mathbb C}(\gamma)[\Lambda:B_d],
 \qquad
 \dim H(d,\gamma)_N
   =\dim_{\mathbb C}(\gamma)[\Gamma:A_d].$$ For the original model these formulas read $$\dim_M E(A,B,\delta,\sigma)
   =\dim_{\mathbb C}(\sigma)[\Lambda:B],
 \qquad
 \dim E(A,B,\delta,\sigma)_N
   =\dim_{\mathbb C}(\sigma)[\Gamma:A].$$ Zero fibres may be omitted. Dimensions add under finite orthogonal sums.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-calculus-dimensions"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-calculus-dimensions</summary>

*Proof.* The free coordinate-orbit identifications in Lemma <a href="#lem:calculus-induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:calculus-induction">7</a> exhibit exactly the stated numbers of copies of the standard left and right modules. Each standard module has dimension one for the normalized factor trace. Lemma <a href="#lem:calculus-graph-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:calculus-graph-model">8</a> gives the original-model formulas. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### All bounded intertwiners and all summands

<a id="stmt-thm-calculus-hom"></a>
<div id="thm:calculus-hom" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:calculus-hom"></a>
**Theorem 10** (Full-germ intertwiner formula). *Choose finitely many distinct $D$-orbits of germs and a representative $d$ in each orbit. Let $\gamma_d,\tau_d$ be finite-dimensional projective representations of $C_d$, with multiplier $\alpha|_{C_d}$; either may be zero. Then $$\label{eq:calculus-hom}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{M\text{--}N}
 \left(\bigoplus_dH(d,\gamma_d),\,
       \bigoplus_dH(d,\tau_d)\right)
 =\bigoplus_d\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C_d,\alpha}(\gamma_d,\tau_d).$$ The equality is implemented by restriction to the representative fibres and extension by transport. Norm is the maximum of the fibre norms, and adjoints and composition are fibrewise. In particular all closed bimodule summands are obtained by taking invariant subspaces of these finite-dimensional fibre representations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-calculus-hom"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-calculus-hom</summary>

<a id="eq:calculus-hs-column"></a>
*Proof.* Realize both modules over their union of full-germ orbits, assigning a zero fibre where necessary. An intertwiner $T$ has finite-dimensional matrix blocks $T_{yx}:V_x\to W_y$. If $(e_i)$ is an orthonormal basis of $V_x$, then $$\label{eq:calculus-hs-column}
 \sum_y\|T_{yx}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_i\|Te_i\|^2
 \leq\dim(V_x)\|T\|^2<\infty.$$ Equivariance identifies $T_{sy,sx}$ with $U^\tau_s(y)T_{yx}U^\gamma_s(x)^*$. Its Hilbert–Schmidt norm is therefore unchanged. For $s\in C_x$, Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-hs-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-hs-column">[eq:calculus-hs-column]</a> implies that a nonzero block $T_{yx}$ can occur only when the $C_x$-orbit of $y$ is finite. By Lemma <a href="#lem:calculus-germ-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:calculus-germ-test">6</a>, this forces $y=x$. Thus every intertwiner is diagonal in the germ labels, including between initially different orbit bundles.

At a representative label $d$, equivariance is exactly the requirement that $T_d$ intertwine the two $\alpha|_{C_d}$-projective representations. Conversely, such an operator extends by $$T_{sd}=U^\tau_s(d)T_dU^\gamma_s(d)^*.$$ This is independent of the choice of $s$: changing $s$ by an element of $C_d$ uses the intertwining identity, and the identical scalar multiplier on both sides cancels. The extension is bounded with norm $\|T_d\|$ on that orbit and commutes with every joint group generator. Normality of the endpoint representations then makes it a bimodule intertwiner. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-hom">[eq:calculus-hom]</a> and the norm assertion. The formulas for adjoint and composition follow on each fibre.

A closed bimodule summand is the range of a projection in the bounded bimodule commutant. The preceding description identifies that projection with a family of $C_d$-equivariant fibre projections. Their ranges are precisely the asserted projective-representation summands, and their transported ranges give the entire module summand. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:calculus-summand-dimensions"></a>
Consider any closed bimodule summand $K$ of a finite sum of the original graph models. Regroup each model by Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-regrouping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-regrouping">[eq:calculus-regrouping]</a>, combine copies of the same full-germ orbit, and apply Theorem <a href="#thm:calculus-hom" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:calculus-hom">10</a> to the projection onto $K$. We obtain a finite orthogonal sum of full-germ bundles, with exactly the intertwiners in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-hom" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-hom">[eq:calculus-hom]</a>. In particular, if its fibre representations are $\rho_d$, its actual dimensions are $$\label{eq:calculus-summand-dimensions}
 \dim_M K=\sum_d\dim_{\mathbb C}(\rho_d)[\Lambda:B_d],
 \qquad
 \dim K_N=\sum_d\dim_{\mathbb C}(\rho_d)[\Gamma:A_d].$$ Both are positive integers for $K\ne0$. This conclusion uses the description of its projection; the dimensions of a containing model alone would not imply it.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 11* (Why a presenting graph must be enlarged). Let $M=N=L_\mu(\Gamma)$, with arbitrary $\mu$, and suppose $A<\Gamma$ has finite index $k>1$. Present the identity germ $d$ by $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_A:A\to A$, with the one-dimensional trivial fibre. The multiplier on every diagonal pair is $\mu(a,b)\overline{\mu(a,b)}=1$, so this is a model for any source twist. Its full stabilizer is the diagonal copy of $\Gamma$. Regrouping therefore replaces the presenting fibre by the permutation representation on $\ell^2(\Gamma/A)$.

The constant vectors form an invariant line. Its orthogonal projection has every matrix entry equal to $1/k$, and selects $H(d,1)=L^2(M)$ from the model $E(A,A,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_A,1)$. The selected module has dimensions $(1,1)$, whereas the containing model has dimensions $(k,k)$. On each of the $k$ original one-dimensional fibres above $d$, the diagonal block of the selecting projection is $1/k$, which is not a projection. Grouping those fibres at the full stabilizer is what exposes the invariant line and its actual rank.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Conjugation

<a id="eq:calculus-conjugate-transport"></a>
On the conjugate correspondence $\overline{\mathcal H(C,\gamma)}$ the endpoint groups are swapped. Its subgroup and base fibre are $$C^{\mathrm{op}}=\{(h,g):(g,h)\in C\},\qquad
 \bar\gamma(h,g)\bar\xi=\overline{\gamma(g,h)\xi}.$$ The multiplier is $\omega\boxtimes\bar\mu$, since $\bar\gamma$ conjugates $\alpha$ and swaps its two variables. The label $x$ of a full-germ bundle becomes the inverse germ $x^{-1}$: indeed $$\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)x\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g^{-1})\bigr)^{-1}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g)x^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h^{-1}).$$ With swapped coset representatives, the transport is explicitly $$\label{eq:calculus-conjugate-transport}
 U^{\bar X}_{(h,g)}(\bar x)
   =\overline{U^X_{(g,h)}(x)}.$$ Thus it includes the conjugate of the scalar ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a>. At the module level this equality is the defining identity $v_h\bar\xi u_g^*=\overline{u_g\xi v_h^*}$. For an original graph $\delta:A\to B$, the new graph is $\delta^{-1}:B\to A$ and its fibre representation is $b\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\overline{\sigma(\delta^{-1}(b))}$. Fibre intertwiners are conjugated by the same rule; their adjoints and norms are preserved. Proposition <a href="#prop:calculus-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:calculus-dimensions">9</a> also shows directly that the left and right dimensions are exchanged.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Balanced fusion and its scalar transport

<a id="eq:calculus-middle-transport"></a>
Let $P=L_\nu(\Pi)$. Consider a bundle $\mathcal H_X$ from $(\Gamma,\mu)$ to $(\Lambda,\omega)$ and a bundle $\mathcal H_Y$ from $(\Lambda,\omega)$ to $(\Pi,\nu)$, each constructed as above. Write $V_x$ and $W_y$ for their fibres and $\alpha'=\omega\boxtimes\bar\nu$. Finite direct sums are handled by distribution, so we may first take one transitive bundle at each endpoint. Define <a id="eq:calculus-balanced-labels"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-balanced-labels}
 Z=(X\times Y)/\Lambda,
 \qquad h(x,y)=\bigl((1,h)x,(h,1)y\bigr).$$ Identify the tensor fibres along this action using $$\label{eq:calculus-middle-transport}
 J_h(x,y)=U^X_{(1,h)}(x)\otimes U^Y_{(h,1)}(y).$$ These identifications are genuine unitary group transports, because their multiplier is $\overline{\omega(h_1,h_2)}\omega(h_1,h_2)=1$. The action on labels is free, as already its action on $X$ is free. Each quotient fibre $V_z$ is consequently unambiguously a tensor fibre after choosing one representative of $z$. Put $\mathcal H_Z=\bigoplus_{z\in Z}V_z$, with counting norm at any such representatives. Changing representatives gives a unitary identification by Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-middle-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-middle-transport">[eq:calculus-middle-transport]</a>.

<a id="eq:input-A2"></a>
The outer transport before passage to the quotient is $U^X_{(g,1)}\otimes U^Y_{(1,k)}$. It commutes exactly with every $J_h$: each mixed-coordinate factor of either product cocycle is one. Its multiplier is $\mu\boxtimes\bar\nu$. For an explicit formula choose a representative $(x,y)$ of $z$ and representatives for all quotient labels. There is a unique $h$ such that $$\bigl((g,h)x,(h,k)y\bigr)$$ is the chosen representative of $(g,k)z$. With $s=(g,h)$, $t=(h,k)$, $c=c_X(s,x)$ and $b=c_Y(t,y)$, the quotient transport is $$\label{eq:input-A2}
 \frac{\alpha(s,r_x)\alpha'(t,r_y)}
      {\alpha(r_{sx},c)\alpha'(r_{ty},b)}
       \gamma_X(c)\otimes\gamma_Y(b).$$ It is the product of the two transports in Equation <a href="#eq:input-A1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A1">[eq:input-A1]</a>. There is no additional factor from the compensating element $h$, since its lift is the genuine transport in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-middle-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-middle-transport">[eq:calculus-middle-transport]</a>.

To attach a germ to a quotient label, first recall the map from graph cosets to germs. For $x=(g,h)C_\delta\in X$, put $$d_x=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)[\delta]\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g^{-1}),$$ and define $d_y$ in the same way for $y\in Y$. These are the graph-coset-to-germ maps from the earlier regrouping, applied at their respective endpoints; they need not be injective. The middle action changes the two associated germs by $$(d_x,d_y)\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}
       (\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)d_x,\,d_y\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h^{-1})).$$ Hence $z=[(x,y)]\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}d_y d_x$ is well defined. Distinct quotient labels can still have the same germ; the next proposition explains how their fibres are regrouped.

<a id="stmt-prop-calculus-fusion"></a>
<div id="prop:calculus-fusion" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12** (Fusion as a counting tensor-fibre bundle). *There is a unitary correspondence identification <a id="eq:calculus-fusion-unitary"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-fusion-unitary}
 \mathcal H_X\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_{N}\mathcal H_Y
 \cong\mathcal H_Z$$ under which outer transport is Equation <a href="#eq:input-A2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A2">[eq:input-A2]</a>. The space $Z$ has finitely many outer-group orbits. For each $z=[(x,y)]$, its stabilizer is a finite-index subgroup-isomorphism graph with germ $d_y d_x$. Regrouping these orbits over full stabilizers by Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-regrouping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-regrouping">[eq:calculus-regrouping]</a> gives the full-germ decomposition of the fusion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-calculus-fusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-calculus-fusion</summary>

*Proof.* First we check the Hilbert norm and the relative product explicitly. Every $\xi\in V_x$ is right bounded: the map $$R_\xi:L^2(N)\longrightarrow\mathcal H_X,
 \qquad \widehat n\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\xi n$$ initially defined on the group algebra extends with norm $\|\xi\|$. Indeed the vectors $\xi v_h$ occupy distinct fibres as $h$ varies, and all have norm $\|\xi\|$. Choose one $x_i$ in each right $\Lambda$-orbit of $X$, and an orthonormal basis $(e_{ij})_j$ of $V_{x_i}$. Orthogonality of all their group translates gives $$R_{e_{ij}}^*R_{e_{i'j'}}=\delta_{ii'}\delta_{jj'}1.$$ Their ranges exhaust $\mathcal H_X$, because the translates exhaust every labelled fibre. They therefore form an orthonormal free right $N$ basis, with finitely many elements.

<a id="eq:calculus-relative-inner-product"></a>
The defining inner product of the relative tensor product now gives $$\label{eq:calculus-relative-inner-product}
 \left\langle e_{ij}\otimes_N\eta,
                 e_{i'j'}\otimes_N\eta'\right\rangle
 =\delta_{ii'}\delta_{jj'}\langle\eta,\eta'\rangle.$$ Thus that product is unitarily $\bigoplus_i(V_{x_i}\otimes\mathcal H_Y)$. Every label in $Z$ has a unique representative with first coordinate one of the fixed $x_i$: freeness of the right action on $X$ gives uniqueness of the required balancing element. Consequently the counting tensor-fibre Hilbert sum $\mathcal H_Z$ is exactly this same orthogonal sum. Its map to the relative product sends $\xi\otimes\eta\in V_x\otimes W_y$ to $\xi\otimes_N\eta$. Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-relative-inner-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-relative-inner-product">[eq:calculus-relative-inner-product]</a> proves that it is isometric and onto. It is independent of the representative, since <a id="eq:calculus-balanced-vector"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-balanced-vector}
 (\xi v_h^*)\otimes_N(v_h\eta)
 =\xi\otimes_N(v_h^*v_h\eta)
 =\xi\otimes_N\eta.$$ Applying the outer generators to these vectors proves Equation <a href="#eq:input-A2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A2">[eq:input-A2]</a> at the correspondence level.

For finiteness and the stabilizers we give the explicit double-coset calculation, which also specifies the composed germ and its fibre representation. Write the two graph maps as $$\delta:A\longrightarrow B\leq\Lambda,
 \qquad \varepsilon:C\longrightarrow F\leq\Pi,$$ with $A\leq\Gamma$ and $C\leq\Lambda$ of finite index, and take $X=(\Gamma\times\Lambda)/C_\delta$, $Y=(\Lambda\times\Pi)/C_\varepsilon$. Choose $m$ in the finite double-coset set $B\backslash\Lambda/C$. Represent its outer orbit by $$z_m=[(C_\delta,(m,1)C_\varepsilon)].$$ Outer translation and balancing send an arbitrary pair to the form $$((g,h)C_\delta,(\ell,k)C_\varepsilon)
 \mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}(C_\delta,(h^{-1}\ell,1)C_\varepsilon).$$ Changing the initial coset representatives replaces $h^{-1}\ell$ by $\delta(a)^{-1}h^{-1}\ell c$ for $a\in A$, $c\in C$. Hence these outer orbits are indexed exactly by $B\backslash\Lambda/C$.

<a id="eq:calculus-composed-graph"></a>
An outer element $(a,p)$ stabilizes $z_m$ precisely when some $h$ satisfies $$(a,h)\in C_\delta,
 \qquad (m^{-1}hm,p)\in C_\varepsilon.$$ Its stabilizer is consequently the graph of $$\label{eq:calculus-composed-graph}
 \begin{split}
 A_m&=\delta^{-1}(B\cap mCm^{-1}),\\
 \theta_m&=\varepsilon\circ\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(m^{-1})\circ\delta:A_m\longrightarrow F_m,\\
 F_m&=\varepsilon(C\cap m^{-1}Bm).
 \end{split}$$ Both projections have finite index. In particular <a id="eq:calculus-double-coset-labels"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-double-coset-labels}
 Z\cong\bigsqcup_{m\in B\backslash\Lambda/C}
             (\Gamma\times\Pi)/C_{\theta_m}.$$ Here $C_{\theta_m}$ denotes the graph of the displayed actual map, not necessarily its full-germ stabilizer. At $z_m$ the composite germ is $[\varepsilon\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(m^{-1})\delta]=[\theta_m]$. Its graph lies at finite index in its full stabilizer by Lemma <a href="#lem:calculus-germ-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:calculus-germ-test">6</a>. This proves all the stated regrouping assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:calculus-double-coset-fibre"></a>
<a id="eq:calculus-conjugation-coboundary"></a>
For completeness, the last proof yields an explicit finite double-coset formula without unspecified stabilizer representations. Let $\sigma$ and $\tau$ be the original fibre representations on $A$ and $C$. In the fibre at $z_m$ choose $r_{(m,1)C_\varepsilon}=(m,1)$. For $a\in A_m$, write $b=\delta(a)$ and $c=m^{-1}bm\in C$. Equation <a href="#eq:input-A2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-A2">[eq:input-A2]</a>, with compensating element $h=b$, gives $$\label{eq:calculus-double-coset-fibre}
 \gamma_m(a)=
 q_m(b)\,\sigma(a)\otimes\tau(m^{-1}bm),
 \qquad
 q_m(b)=\frac{\omega(b,m)}{\omega(m,m^{-1}bm)}.$$ The correction has the exact coboundary $$\label{eq:calculus-conjugation-coboundary}
 \frac{q_m(b)q_m(b')}{q_m(bb')}
 =\frac{\omega(b,b')}
 {\omega(m^{-1}bm,m^{-1}b'm)}.$$ To verify this, the twisted group-unit identity $v_bv_m=q_m(b)v_mv_{m^{-1}bm}$ can be multiplied successively for $b,b'$ and compared with the identity for $bb'$. Combining Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-conjugation-coboundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-conjugation-coboundary">[eq:calculus-conjugation-coboundary]</a> with the multipliers of $\sigma$ and $\tau$ shows that $\gamma_m$ has multiplier $\mu|_{A_m}/\theta_m^*\nu$. Hence <a id="eq:calculus-double-coset-fusion"></a>$$\label{eq:calculus-double-coset-fusion}
 E(A,B,\delta,\sigma)\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_N
 E(C,F,\varepsilon,\tau)
 \cong\bigoplus_{m\in B\backslash\Lambda/C}
      E(A_m,F_m,\theta_m,\gamma_m).$$ The appropriate endpoint algebras in the second model and in each summand are understood from their displayed groups. One then applies finite projective induction to the full stabilizers and combines any equal full-germ orbits. Formula <a href="#eq:calculus-double-coset-fibre" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-double-coset-fibre">[eq:calculus-double-coset-fibre]</a> exhibits explicitly why replacing the twisted fusion rule by its ordinary version would lose a phase.

The indices in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-composed-graph" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-composed-graph">[eq:calculus-composed-graph]</a> satisfy $$[\Gamma:A_m]=[\Gamma:A][B:B\cap mCm^{-1}],
 \qquad
 [\Pi:F_m]=[\Pi:F][C:C\cap m^{-1}Bm].$$ Counting the left $B$-orbits in $\Lambda/C$ and the right $C$-orbits in $B\backslash\Lambda$ gives, respectively, $$\sum_m[B:B\cap mCm^{-1}]=[\Lambda:C],
 \qquad
 \sum_m[C:C\cap m^{-1}Bm]=[\Lambda:B].$$ Thus both coordinate groups have finitely many orbits in the fused label space. These formulas also verify, directly from the models and their counting norms, the usual multiplicativity of the left dimensions and of the right dimensions under this fusion.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Maps, associativity, and completion of the calculus

The preceding identifications respect the entire operator calculus, not only the isomorphism classes of objects. For the map calculation, first regroup the inputs into full-germ bundles. Theorem <a href="#thm:calculus-hom" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:calculus-hom">10</a> then represents every bounded intertwiner by an equivariant family of fibre maps. An equivariant family $T_x:V_x\to V'_x$ and an equivariant family $S_y:W_y\to W'_y$ give the fibre maps $T_x\otimes S_y$. They commute with the middle identifications in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-middle-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-middle-transport">[eq:calculus-middle-transport]</a>, so descend to the quotient fibres. Under the unitary of Proposition <a href="#prop:calculus-fusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:calculus-fusion">12</a>, they are precisely $T\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_N S$, as is seen on $\xi\otimes_N\eta$. Their norms are bounded by $\|T\|\|S\|$; adjoints and compositions agree fibrewise. Regrouping by finite projective induction is a regrouping of these same Hilbert fibres and maps. Conjugation has the map formula already given in Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-conjugate-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-conjugate-transport">[eq:calculus-conjugate-transport]</a>.

For three bundles, both orders of fusion use the quotient of the triple label space by the two middle groups. Their actions commute: on the shared middle bundle they are its commuting coordinate actions, and their projective factors cancel with those in the adjacent bundles. The fibre identification between the two orders is the ordinary unitary $$(\xi\otimes\eta)\otimes\zeta
       \mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\xi\otimes(\eta\otimes\zeta).$$ The counting norms and Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-relative-inner-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-relative-inner-product">[eq:calculus-relative-inner-product]</a> show that it is also the canonical associativity unitary for the relative products. All scalar identifications are those of Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-middle-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-middle-transport">[eq:calculus-middle-transport]</a>; hence this assertion requires no change of cocycle. For any further number of factors these maps agree on elementary tensors, which are total, so the associativity identifications are coherent. The unit correspondence $L^2(M)$ is the identity-germ model with its one-dimensional trivial graph representation; its unit maps are ordinary left and right multiplication on the corresponding relative tensors.

Conjugation reverses a balanced product. The unitary $$\overline{\mathcal H_X\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_N\mathcal H_Y}
 \longrightarrow
 \overline{\mathcal H_Y}\mathbin{\bar\otimes}_N
       \overline{\mathcal H_X},
 \qquad
 \overline{\xi\otimes_N\eta}
       \mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF27FC> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginallongmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\bar\eta\otimes_N\bar\xi$$ is well defined on tensors of vectors in labelled fibres: the simultaneous middle transport becomes the same transport on the swapped conjugate fibres, with both scalar factors conjugated and their product still one. The counting tensor-fibre model of Proposition <a href="#prop:calculus-fusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:calculus-fusion">12</a> proves preservation of the norm: the map bijects the quotient labels and is unitary on each conjugate tensor fibre. These tensors are total. It intertwines the swapped endpoint actions and the conjugated fibre maps. Thus the conjugation, unit, and associativity identifications agree with those of the correspondence category at the level of actual operators.

We now apply the geometric input. Proposition <a href="#prop:recovery-exhaustion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:recovery-exhaustion">5</a> places every bifinite correspondence with source in $\mathscr K$ inside a finite sum of graph models. The preceding full-germ calculation resolves its selecting projection into fibre projections. Together with the operation formulas just proved, this establishes every assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:correspondences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:correspondences">4</a>, including all bounded intertwiners, actual summands and their dimensions, and the specified conjugation and fusion maps. Throughout the section only the matched-subgroup ratio is required to have a finite-dimensional projective realization. None of the calculations imposes a finite-image condition on that representation or a finite-type condition on either individual endpoint cocycle.

<a id="sec:endpoints"></a>
### Arbitrary factor endpoints and stable canonical recovery

We now use the correspondence classification to recover the multiplication of an unknown factor. The argument first realizes that factor, up to amplification, as a finite-index extension of the given twisted group factor. Its self-correspondence grading then consists of bounded operators and respects the actual product. This additional multiplication step is what turns the correspondence data into a group presentation. The homogeneous-product and minimal-corner construction has a direct predecessor in Donvil–Vaes (Donvil and Vaes 2025, Proposition 6.5 and Theorem 6.6) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[DonvilVaes2025](#ref-DonvilVaes2025)]. Their construction already permits arbitrary source cocycles and recovers the exact matched ratio. We adapt it from globally defined automorphism degrees to finite-index isomorphism germs.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A finite-index extension and its intrinsic grading

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoints-extension"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoints-extension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (From a bifinite neighbor to an extension). *Let $M,P$ be $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors and let ${}_M K_P\ne0$ be bifinite. The commutant $Q$ of the right $P$-action is a positive finite amplification of $P$, and the given left action identifies $M$ with a unital finite-index subfactor of $Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-extension</summary>

*Proof.* The finite right module has a column realization $K=pL^2(P)^n$, with $r=(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_n\otimes\tau_P)(p)=\dim K_P>0$. Its right-action commutant is $Q=p(M_n(\mathbb C)\bar\otimes P)p=P^r$, equipped with the normalized corner trace. The left action of $M$ is a normal unital embedding into $Q$, because a nonzero representation of a factor has zero kernel. Its restriction preserves normalized trace.

As a left $Q$-module, $K$ has finite positive dimension $1/r$. Choose an integer $j$ with $j/r\geq1$. Comparison of finite module projections embeds $L^2(Q)$ as a left $Q$-submodule of $K^{\oplus j}$. Restriction to $M$ makes it a finite left $M$-module. Thus the Jones index $[Q:M]=\dim_M L^2(Q)$ is finite. This uses the standard finite-factor column and index identifications; see (Jones 1983) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Jones1983](#ref-Jones1983)] and (Jones 2009, Theorem 10.1.1, Proposition 10.1.3, and Theorem 10.2.1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Jones2009](#ref-Jones2009)]. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:endpoints-inner-germs"></a>
Fix $\Gamma\in\mathscr K$, an arbitrary normalized scalar cocycle $\mu$, and $M=L_\mu(\Gamma)$. Write $D=\mathop{\mathrm{Comm}}(\Gamma)$ for the group of virtual automorphism germs defined in Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">3</a>. The map $$\iota:\Gamma\longrightarrow D,\qquad
 \iota(g)=[\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g)],$$ is injective: an element in its kernel centralizes a finite-index subgroup of $\Gamma$, and hence has a finite conjugacy class. For a representative $\delta:A\to B$ of $d\in D$, one has $$d\,\iota(a)\,d^{-1}=\iota(\delta(a))\qquad(a\in A).
 \label{eq:endpoints-inner-germs}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoints-grading"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoints-grading" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:input-B1"></a>
**Lemma 14** (The intrinsic self-correspondence grading). *Every bifinite $M$–$M$ correspondence has an orthogonal decomposition $$K=\bigoplus_{d\in D}K_d$$ into finite-dimensional spaces, with only countably many nonzero terms, such that $$u_gK_du_h^*=K_{\iota(g)d\iota(h)^{-1}}.$$ The support is contained in finitely many $\iota(\Gamma)$-double cosets. For every representative $\delta:A\to B$ of $d$, $$K_d=\left\{\xi\in K:
  \dim_{\mathbb C}\operatorname{span}
    \{u_{\delta(a)}\xi u_a^*:a\in A\}<\infty\right\}.
 \label{eq:input-B1}$$ In particular, for the fixed presentation $M=L_\mu(\Gamma)$, the grading is determined by the two actions and does not depend on a choice of arithmetic model decomposition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-grading"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-grading</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:correspondences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:correspondences">4</a> and the full-germ decomposition of Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">3</a>. Use the inverse of its germ labels, so that our degree is a germ from the right group to the left group. If $x$ is an old label and $d=x^{-1}$, the old action gives $$\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(h)x\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(g^{-1})\bigr)^{-1}
 =\iota(g)d\iota(h)^{-1}.$$ Combining the finitely many representation fibres at each identical inverse label gives $K_d$ and the stated covariance. The finitely many old germ orbits become finitely many $\iota(\Gamma)$-double cosets. Separability makes the nonzero support countable.

For a representative $\delta$, the operators $U_a\xi=u_{\delta(a)}\xi u_a^*$ preserve $K_d$. Their multiplier is $\mu(\delta(a),\delta(b))\overline{\mu(a,b)}$. They form a projective unitary representation, so its orbit span is invariant, even when the multiplier is not trivial. This proves the forward inclusion in Equation <a href="#eq:input-B1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-B1">[eq:input-B1]</a>.

Conversely let $W$ be a finite-dimensional invariant orbit span and choose an orthonormal basis $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_l$ of $W$. If $P_x$ projects onto $K_x$, the numbers $$m(x)=\sum_{i=1}^l\|P_x\xi_i\|^2$$ have finite sum $l$ and are invariant under $x\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\iota(\delta(a))x\iota(a)^{-1}$. Indeed $U_a$ permutes the degree spaces and carries the chosen basis of $W$ to another orthonormal basis. Positive mass is therefore possible only on a finite orbit. The stabilizer of such an $x$ contains a finite-index subgroup $A_0\leq A$, and $$x\iota(a)x^{-1}=\iota(\delta(a))\qquad(a\in A_0).$$ Represent $x$ by $\epsilon:C\to E$ and restrict to $A_0\cap C$. Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-inner-germs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-inner-germs">[eq:endpoints-inner-germs]</a> and injectivity of $\iota$ give $\epsilon(a)=\delta(a)$ there. Thus $x=d$. All of $W$ lies in $K_d$, proving the converse and the asserted intrinsic description. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Bounded homogeneous operators and the minimal corner

Let $M\subset Q$ now be a unital finite-index inclusion of $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-grading" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-grading">14</a> to ${}_M L^2(Q)_M$. Until the next lemma, its degree spaces are only Hilbert subspaces of $L^2(Q)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoints-bounded-grades"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoints-bounded-grades" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:endpoints-grade-product"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Boundedness and multiplication). *Every $K_d$ consists of bounded elements of $Q$, and $$K_dK_f\subset K_{df},\qquad K_d^*=K_{d^{-1}},\qquad
 1\in K_1,\qquad u_a\in K_{\iota(a)}.
 \label{eq:endpoints-grade-product}$$ Consequently $K_1$ is a finite-dimensional unital $*$-subalgebra of $Q$, normalized by the $u_a$, and $\tau_Q(K_d)=0$ for $d\ne1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-bounded-grades"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-bounded-grades</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\xi\in K_d$. For distinct $g\in\Gamma$, the right translates $\xi u_g$ have distinct degrees $d\iota(g)$. Injectivity of $\iota$ therefore gives $\langle\xi,\xi u_g\rangle=0$ for $g\ne e$. Initially put $a=\xi^*\xi\in L^1(Q)_+$. The $L^1$ extension of the trace expectation satisfies $$E_M(a)=\|\xi\|_2^2 1:$$ indeed, for each $g\in\Gamma$, $$\tau_M(E_M(a)u_g)=\tau_Q(a u_g)=\langle\xi,\xi u_g\rangle.$$ These values agree with those of $\|\xi\|_2^2 1$. They determine the normal functional associated to $E_M(a)\in L^1(M)$, because the group algebra is ultraweakly dense in $M$. For the bounded spectral truncations $a_n=\min(a,n)$, Pimsner–Popa’s finite-index inequality (Pimsner and Popa 1986, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[PimsnerPopa1986](#ref-PimsnerPopa1986)] gives $$[Q:M]^{-1}a_n\leq E_M(a_n)\leq E_M(a)=\|\xi\|_2^2 1.$$ Monotone convergence makes $a$ bounded, and hence $\xi\in Q$, with $\|\xi\|_\infty\leq[Q:M]^{1/2}\|\xi\|_2$. This argument uses orthogonality of the degrees, so the scalar phases in the right action do not change it. We now verify the product itself.

Take $\xi\in K_d$, $\eta\in K_f$, and representatives $\delta:A\to B$, $\epsilon:C\to E$. On the finite-index domain $C_0=\{a\in C:\epsilon(a)\in A\}$, the exact identity $$u_{\delta\epsilon(a)}\xi\eta u_a^*
   =(u_{\delta\epsilon(a)}\xi u_{\epsilon(a)}^*)
      (u_{\epsilon(a)}\eta u_a^*)$$ puts the orbit of the product into the finite-dimensional span of products of bases of $K_d$ and $K_f$. These are bounded elements, as just proved. The intrinsic test <a href="#eq:input-B1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-B1">[eq:input-B1]</a> yields $\xi\eta\in K_{df}$. Taking adjoints in the same test gives $K_d^*\subset K_{d^{-1}}$, and taking adjoints again gives equality. The vector $1$ is fixed by the identity graph. For the graph of $\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(a)$, the vector $u_a$ is carried to a scalar multiple of itself, since the corresponding product of group labels is $a$. Thus its graph orbit span is one-dimensional, even with the twist. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-grade-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-grade-product">[eq:endpoints-grade-product]</a>.

Finite dimension of $K_1$ comes from the grading lemma, and normalization follows from its covariance. Orthogonality to $1\in K_1$ gives the trace assertion for other degrees. ◻

</details>

Compressing $K_1$ to a scalar algebra will now force each surviving degree to be a unitary line.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoints-minimal-corner"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoints-minimal-corner" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (The minimal identity corner). *Let $p$ be a minimal projection of $K_1$. There are a countable subgroup $\Lambda\leq D$ and a normalized scalar cocycle $\omega$ such that the actual corner $pQp$, with normalized trace, is isomorphic to $L_\omega(\Lambda)$. Each nonzero space $pK_dp$ is one-dimensional and has a unitary basis vector in the corner.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-minimal-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-minimal-corner</summary>

*Proof.* Left and right multiplication by $p$ are commuting orthogonal projections on $L^2(Q)$. They preserve each degree by Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-grade-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-grade-product">[eq:endpoints-grade-product]</a>; their product projects onto $L^2(pQp)$. Thus, up to the common normalization factor of the corner trace, $$L^2(pQp)=\bigoplus_{d\in D}pK_dp,\qquad pK_1p=\mathbb Cp.$$ For $0\ne x\in pK_dp$, the product and adjoint laws give $x^*x=\alpha p$ and $xx^*=\beta p$, with positive scalars. Traciality gives $\alpha=\beta$. Therefore $x/\sqrt\alpha$ is a unitary in $pQp$. If $y$ belongs to the same degree, $x^*y\in\mathbb Cp$, so $y$ is a scalar multiple of $x$.

Set $\Lambda=\{d:pK_dp\ne0\}$. It contains the identity, is closed under inverses, and is closed under products because products of its homogeneous unitaries cannot vanish. It is countable by orthogonality and separability. Choose unitary vectors $v_d\in pK_dp$, with $v_1=p$, and define $$v_dv_f=\omega(d,f)v_{df}.$$ Unitarity makes the coefficient unimodular, associativity gives the 2-cocycle identity, and $v_1=p$ gives normalization. The $v_d$ form an orthonormal basis for the normalized corner trace. The unitary $\delta_d\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}v_d$ from $\ell^2(\Lambda)$ to $L^2(pQp)$ intertwines twisted left regular multiplication with left multiplication by these units.

Let $Q_0$ be the von Neumann algebra they generate. The trace-preserving expectation onto $Q_0$ has $L^2$-range containing every $v_d$, so it has full range and $Q_0=pQp$. The basis unitary therefore gives a normal unital complex-linear $*$-isomorphism $L_\omega(\Lambda)\cong pQp$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The ratio condition and the reconstructed group

The minimal corner has supplied a twisted group algebra, but the group $\Lambda$ still needs to satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:endpoints">3</a>. We next recover a finite-index copy of a subgroup of $\Gamma$ inside $\Lambda$, obtain the projective ratio from the identity matrix block, and prove that $\Lambda$ is ICC.

<a id="stmt-prop-endpoints-corner-constraints"></a>
<div id="prop:endpoints-corner-constraints" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Arithmetic constraints on the corner). *For the corner in Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-minimal-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-minimal-corner">16</a>, the group $\Lambda$ is countably infinite and ICC. There are finite-index subgroups $A\leq\Gamma$, $B\leq\Lambda$, an actual group isomorphism $\delta:A\to B$, and a finite-dimensional projective representation with multiplier $\mu|_A/\delta^*(\omega|_B)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endpoints-corner-constraints"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endpoints-corner-constraints</summary>

*Proof.* Let $z$ be the central support of $p$ in the finite-dimensional algebra $K_1$, so that $zK_1$ is the matrix block containing $p$. We first split the action on this block from the homogeneous unitaries that will survive in $pQp$. The subgroup $$A=\{a\in\Gamma:u_azu_a^*=z\}$$ has finite index, because conjugation permutes the finitely many central blocks. Although these conjugations preserve $z$, they may move $p$ inside its matrix block. We therefore remove their action on that block before compressing. Choose unitary matrix implementers $s_a\in\mathcal U(zK_1)$, $s_e=z$, for $\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(u_a)|_{zK_1}$. Since these automorphisms form an ordinary group action, there is a normalized scalar cocycle $\kappa$ with $$s_as_b=\kappa(a,b)s_{ab}.$$ These matrices already constitute a finite-dimensional projective representation. Their image is allowed to be infinite.

The unitaries $q_a=s_a^*u_az\in zQz$ commute with $zK_1$. Using $u_as_b^*u_a^*=s_as_b^*s_a^*$, their product is $$\begin{aligned}
 q_aq_b
  &=s_a^*(u_as_b^*u_a^*)u_au_bz\\
  &=\mu(a,b)s_b^*s_a^*u_{ab}z
    =\mu(a,b)\overline{\kappa(a,b)}q_{ab}.
\end{aligned}$$ They are homogeneous of degree $\iota(a)$, and they commute with $p$. Their compressions $t_a=pq_ap$ are therefore corner unitaries in $pK_{\iota(a)}p$, satisfying the same product formula. It follows that $B=\iota(A)$ lies in $\Lambda$, and that $\delta=\iota|_A:A\to B$ is an actual isomorphism.

<a id="eq:endpoints-ratio"></a>
To compare their multiplier with the cocycle already chosen in Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-minimal-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-minimal-corner">16</a>, write the basis unitary of degree $\iota(a)$ as $v_{\iota(a)}=\lambda(a)t_a$, where $\lambda:A\to\mathbb T$ and $\lambda(e)=1$. With the convention $$(\partial\lambda)(a,b)=\lambda(a)\lambda(b)\lambda(ab)^{-1},$$ we obtain $$\delta^*\omega=(\partial\lambda)\mu|_A\,\overline\kappa,
 \qquad
 \frac{\mu|_A}{\delta^*\omega}
       =(\partial\lambda)^{-1}\kappa.
 \label{eq:endpoints-ratio}$$ On the defining space of the block, the matrices $a\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\overline{\lambda(a)}s_a$ realize precisely the second multiplier. This is the required ratio condition.

We have the subgroup isomorphism and its exact ratio. It remains to show that its image has finite index in $\Lambda$ and that $\Lambda$ is ICC. Put $H=\iota(\Gamma)$. Every $d\in D$ commensurates $H$, by Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-inner-germs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-inner-germs">[eq:endpoints-inner-germs]</a> on a finite-index domain. Consequently each double coset $HdH$ is a finite union of right $H$-cosets. Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-grading" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-grading">14</a> puts the original degree support in finitely many such double cosets. Since $B$ has finite index in $H$, that support is contained in finitely many right $B$-cosets. The subgroup $\Lambda$ is contained in this support and contains $B$; hence $[\Lambda:B]<\infty$. In particular it is infinite.

Suppose $l\in\Lambda$ has finite conjugacy class. Its centralizer $C_\Lambda(l)$ has finite index in $\Lambda$. Thus $A_0=\iota^{-1}(C_\Lambda(l)\cap B)$ has finite index in $\Gamma$, and $l$ centralizes $\iota(A_0)$. Represent its germ by $\epsilon:C\to E$. For $a\in A_0\cap C$, centralization and Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-inner-germs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-inner-germs">[eq:endpoints-inner-germs]</a> give $\iota(\epsilon(a))=\iota(a)$. Injectivity of $\iota$ gives $\epsilon(a)=a$ on this finite-index subgroup. Thus $l=1$ in $D$, proving ICC. The construction uses only subgroup isomorphisms and introduces no finite kernel. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endpoints-corner-constraints-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endpoints-corner-constraints</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:endpoints">3</a>.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-extension">13</a> to the given nonzero bifinite correspondence. It gives a finite-index extension $M\subset Q=P^r$. The preceding lemmas and Proposition <a href="#prop:endpoints-corner-constraints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:endpoints-corner-constraints">17</a> give $pQp\cong L_\omega(\Lambda)$ with the asserted group and ratio data. Since $pQp$ is the amplification $P^{r\tau_Q(p)}$, standard column equivalences give $$P\cong L_\omega(\Lambda)^t,
 \qquad t=(r\tau_Q(p))^{-1}>0.$$

Conversely the stated subgroup and projective data give a nonzero bifinite arithmetic correspondence from $M$ to $L_\omega(\Lambda)$, by Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">3</a>. Compose it with the corner equivalence from that factor to its amplification and then with the specified factor isomorphism to $P$. This is a nonzero bifinite $M$–$P$ correspondence.

When $\mu=1$, conjugating the finite-dimensional ratio representation and transporting it through $\delta$ gives a representation with multiplier $\omega|_B$. Projective induction from $B$ to $\Lambda$, with the given global multiplier $\omega$, is finite-dimensional and realizes $\omega$ itself; compare (Donvil and Vaes 2025, Lemmas 6.7–6.8) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[DonvilVaes2025](#ref-DonvilVaes2025)]. The induction formula of Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">3</a> applies equally to this finite-index subgroup. For general $\mu$ the construction proves only the exact ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-ratio">[eq:endpoints-ratio]</a>, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Stable scales and the actual isomorphism

For a specified stable isomorphism, the category gives a different constraint: its equivalence correspondence has reciprocal dimensions. After integrality forces both dimensions to be one, we will use the given corner columns to identify the specified map. All dimensions below use the normalized factor traces and the ordinary unnormalized matrix trace $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_n$, with $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_n(1)=n$.

<a id="stmt-lem-endpoints-equivalence-dimensions"></a>
<div id="lem:endpoints-equivalence-dimensions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Dimension and corner equivalences). *For composable bifinite correspondences of finite factors, left dimensions multiply under relative tensor product, and so do right dimensions. If $M^t=p(M_n(\mathbb C)\bar\otimes M)p$ and $C_p=pL^2(M)^n$, then ${}_{M^t}C_p{}_M$ is an equivalence correspondence with dimensions $(1/t,t)$ in left/right order.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-equivalence-dimensions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-equivalence-dimensions</summary>

*Proof.* For the right-dimension assertion, realize a right $N$-module $H$ as $eL^2(N)^k$. Then $H\otimes_N K=eK^k$. The normalized trace on the finite factor $\operatorname{End}(K_P)$ restricts to the normalized trace on its unital copy of $N$. Computing the dimension of $eK^k$ therefore gives $$\dim(H\otimes_N K)_P
  =(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_k\otimes\tau_N)(e)\,\dim K_P.$$ The left formula follows by conjugating the correspondences. The column $C_p$ has right dimension $t$ by its defining projection. The standard column pairings identify $C_p\otimes_M\overline{C_p}$ with $L^2(M^t)$ and $\overline{C_p}\otimes_{M^t}C_p$ with $L^2(M)$. The multiplication formula makes its left dimension $1/t$. These identifications use the normalized corner trace, which accounts for the reciprocal scale. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-endpoints-equivalence-dimensions-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-endpoints-equivalence-dimensions</summary>

<a id="eq:endpoints-lifted-isomorphism"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>.* Set $M=L_\omega(\Lambda)$ and $N=L_\nu(\Pi)$. Use the actual given corner presentations $$M^t=pM_n(M)p,\qquad N^s=qM_m(N)q,$$ and the columns $C_p=pL^2(M)^n$, $C_q=qL^2(N)^m$. A specified isomorphism $\theta:M^t\to N^s$ gives the $M$–$N$ equivalence correspondence $$H_\theta=\overline{C_p}\otimes_{M^t}
 {}_\theta L^2(N^s)\otimes_{N^s}C_q.
 \label{eq:endpoints-lifted-isomorphism}$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-equivalence-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-equivalence-dimensions">18</a>, its left and right dimensions are $t/s$ and $s/t$. Theorem <a href="#thm:correspondences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:correspondences">4</a> applies to $H_\theta$ because $\Lambda\in\mathscr K$ and $\Pi$ is countably infinite ICC. Its full-germ decomposition and Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-summand-dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-summand-dimensions">[eq:calculus-summand-dimensions]</a> make both numbers positive integers. Their product is one, so $t=s$.

The same formula now forces a single full-germ orbit, a one-dimensional fibre, and both stabilizer projections equal to the whole endpoint groups. Indeed each nonzero orbit contributes its positive integer fibre dimension times a positive integer subgroup index to each of the two sums, both of which equal one. Thus its graph is an actual group isomorphism $\delta:\Lambda\to\Pi$. Consequently $\Pi\in\mathscr K$, since $\Lambda\in\mathscr K$ and this class is invariant under group isomorphism. The one-dimensional fibre is a normalized cochain $b:\Lambda\to
\mathbb T$ with $$\omega(g,h)=b(g)b(h)b(gh)^{-1}\nu(\delta(g),\delta(h)).$$ The arithmetic bundle identification in Section <a href="#sec:calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:calculus">3</a> therefore gives $H_\theta\cong {}_\varphi L^2(N)$, where $$\varphi(u_g)=b(g)v_{\delta(g)}.$$ Multiplication and the displayed cochain equation define a $*$-homomorphism on the group algebra. Its trace-preserving basis unitary extends it to a normal unital isomorphism $M\to N$.

We now return to the specified map in its actual corners. First choose any partial isometry between the corresponding projections; then the correspondence identification will supply the inner correction that makes it implement $\theta$. The projections $\varphi^{(n)}(p)$ and $q$ have equal unnormalized trace $t=s$. Comparison of projections in the finite matrix amplification of $N$ gives a rectangular partial isometry $$z\in M_{m,n}(N),\qquad
 z^*z=\varphi^{(n)}(p),\qquad zz^*=q.$$ Define $\theta_0(x)=z\varphi^{(n)}(x)z^*$ on $pM_n(M)p$. Its lifted correspondence in Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-lifted-isomorphism" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-lifted-isomorphism">[eq:endpoints-lifted-isomorphism]</a> is ${}_\varphi L^2(N)$. To check this directly, on bounded columns set $F(\xi)=z\varphi^{(n)}(\xi)$, applying $\varphi$ entrywise. For $x\in pM_n(M)p$, $a\in M$, and $\xi,\eta\in pM^{n}$, one has $$F(x\xi a)=\theta_0(x)F(\xi)\varphi(a),\qquad
 F(\xi)^*F(\eta)=\varphi(\xi^*\eta).$$ Moreover $F$ is onto the bounded columns $qN^m$, since $zz^*=q$. These identities, followed by the normalized column pairings of Lemma <a href="#lem:endpoints-equivalence-dimensions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:endpoints-equivalence-dimensions">18</a>, contract the two column factors to the trace module with left action $\varphi$.

<a id="eq:endpoints-actual-corner-map"></a>
Consequently $H_\theta\cong H_{\theta_0}$. Tensor on the left with $C_p$ and on the right with $\overline{C_q}$; the column equivalences cancel and give a unitary $$U:{}_\theta L^2(N^s)\longrightarrow {}_{\theta_0}L^2(N^s).$$ A right-linear unitary on this standard trace module is left multiplication by a unitary $w\in N^s$. Its left intertwining identity says $w\theta(x)=\theta_0(x)w$. Replacing $z$ by $w^*z$, which has the same initial and final projections, therefore yields the actual formula $$\theta(x)=z\varphi^{(n)}(x)z^*
 \qquad(x\in pM_n(M)p).
 \label{eq:endpoints-actual-corner-map}$$ At ordinary unit scale this is precisely $\theta(u_g)=z\,b(g)v_{\delta(g)}z^*$ with $z\in N$ unitary.

Conversely a group isomorphism and normalized cochain satisfying the cocycle equation construct $\varphi$ by the same basis unitary. At equal scales, comparison of the two actual projections supplies $z$, and Equation <a href="#eq:endpoints-actual-corner-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoints-actual-corner-map">[eq:endpoints-actual-corner-map]</a> constructs the corner isomorphism. This proves both directions and the specified-map assertion. Taking identical factor inputs and scales $1,t$ shows that every factor in the statement has fundamental group $\{1\}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:examples"></a>
### Commensurability, named lattices, and endomorphisms

The category calculation gives concise consequences with different mathematical content. Untwisted correspondence existence detects abstract commensurability. Stable isomorphism recovers an entire group and a specified map. A finite-index endomorphism requires one additional classical covolume argument; its two module dimensions are not initially reciprocal.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Untwisted correspondences and finite-type neighbors

<a id="stmt-cor-commensurability"></a>
<div id="cor:commensurability" class="corollary">

**Corollary 19**. *Let $\Gamma\in\mathscr K$ and let $\Lambda$ be any countably infinite ICC group. There is a nonzero bifinite $L(\Gamma)$–$L(\Lambda)$ correspondence if and only if $\Gamma$ and $\Lambda$ are abstractly commensurable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-commensurability"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-commensurability</summary>

*Proof.* A nonzero correspondence has a nonzero full-germ orbit in Theorem <a href="#thm:correspondences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:correspondences">4</a>. Its stabilizer is the graph of an actual isomorphism between finite-index subgroups. Conversely such an isomorphism, with its one-dimensional trivial fibre representation, gives the model in Equation <a href="#eq:intro-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-model">[eq:intro-model]</a>. ◻

</details>

With the finite-type convention of Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, the endpoint theorem specializes as follows.

<a id="stmt-cor-untwisted-neighbors"></a>
<div id="cor:untwisted-neighbors" class="corollary">

**Corollary 20**. *For $\Gamma\in\mathscr K$, a separable $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor $P$ is joined to $L(\Gamma)$ by a nonzero bifinite correspondence if and only if $P\cong L_\omega(\Lambda)^t$ for $t>0$, an ICC group $\Lambda$ abstractly commensurable with $\Gamma$, and a finite-type cocycle $\omega$ on $\Lambda$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-untwisted-neighbors"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-untwisted-neighbors</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:endpoints">3</a> and the final projective-induction argument in its proof give necessity. For sufficiency, restrict a projective representation of multiplier $\omega$ to the matched subgroup and conjugate it; its multiplier is exactly $1/\delta^*\omega$. The graph model and the amplification equivalence give the required correspondence. ◻

</details>

For arbitrary source twist this specialization must be replaced by the ratio in Theorem <a href="#thm:endpoints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:endpoints">3</a>. In particular, its direct proof admits the identity correspondence of $L_\mu(\Gamma)$ whatever the individual cocycle $\mu$ may be.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Examples and specified canonical maps

The familiar arithmetic examples are $\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$, its principal congruence subgroup $$\Gamma(3)=\ker\bigl(\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)
                    \longrightarrow\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z)\bigr),$$ all their finite-index subgroups, $\mathrm{PSL}_n(\mathbb Z)$ for $n\geq4$, and $\mathrm{PSp}_4(\mathbb Z)$, with the latter two symbols denoting the quotient by the center of the integral matrix group. The arithmetic lattice theorem places these projective groups in the corresponding center-free real groups (Borel and Harish-Chandra 1962) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BorelHarishChandra1962](#ref-BorelHarishChandra1962)]; the finite-covolume statement appears also in (Borel and Harish-Chandra 1961, Theorem 1(iv)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[BorelHarishChandra1961](#ref-BorelHarishChandra1961)]. For even $n$, $\mathrm{PSL}_n(\mathbb R)$ is the identity root-generated subgroup of $\mathrm{PGL}_n(\mathbb R)$; for odd $n$ it is all of it. Likewise $\mathrm{PSp}_4(\mathbb R)$ is the root-generated adjoint symplectic group. These ambient groups have real rank at least two and property $(T)$ (Bekka, Harpe, and Valette 2008) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[BHV2008](#ref-BHV2008)].

These projective lattices are ICC. A finite conjugacy class gives a finite-index centralizer. That subgroup is Zariski dense by Borel density, so the element centralizes the connected ambient algebraic group and is trivial in its adjoint form (Borel 1960) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Borel1960](#ref-Borel1960)]. Equivalently, the centralizer formulation in (Furstenberg 1976, Corollary 3) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Furstenberg1976](#ref-Furstenberg1976)] applies directly to the finite-index lattice and gives the ambient center. For $\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$ the real center is already trivial. A finite-index subgroup of an ICC group remains ICC: its finite conjugacy class would yield a finite class in the ambient group after using finitely many coset representatives. Thus every group just listed belongs to $\mathscr K$.

For any such group $\Gamma$, any countably infinite ICC group $\Lambda$, and any specified isomorphism $$\theta:L(\Gamma)\longrightarrow pM_k(L(\Lambda))p,$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a> yields $$(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_k\otimes\tau)(p)=1,\qquad
 \theta(u_g)=\chi(g)Wv_{\delta(g)}W^*,\qquad
 W^*W=1,\quad WW^*=p,$$ where $W\in M_{k,1}(L(\Lambda))$, $\delta:\Gamma\to\Lambda$ is an actual group isomorphism, and $\chi:\Gamma\to\mathbb T$ is a character. This includes the specified column in the specified corner. The character becomes the cochain of Equation <a href="#eq:intro-cochain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-cochain">[eq:intro-cochain]</a> when scalar twists are present. In particular all the corresponding twisted factors have fundamental group $\{1\}$.

Definition <a href="#def:class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:class">1</a> also includes ICC lattices in the permitted finite-place groups, mixed real and finite-place products, and products with quaternionic or Cayley real factors of property $(T)$. Finite products of the preceding discrete examples give reducible cases: a nonidentity element has a nonidentity coordinate with infinite conjugacy class. More generally the companion exhaustion theorem already allows arbitrary reducible lattices, nonuniform lattices, and torsion. No repetition of the individual geometric proofs is required for these examples.

<a id="stmt-cor-outer-automorphisms"></a>
<div id="cor:outer-automorphisms" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (Outer automorphisms). *For $\Gamma\in\mathscr K$, there is an isomorphism of abstract groups $$\operatorname{Out}(L(\Gamma))
 \cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(\Gamma,\mathbb T)\rtimes\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma),
 \qquad [\delta]\cdot\chi=\chi\circ\delta^{-1},$$ where $\operatorname{Out}$ denotes automorphisms modulo inner automorphisms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-outer-automorphisms"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-outer-automorphisms</summary>

*Proof.* Put $T_\chi(u_g)=\chi(g)u_g$ and $U_\delta(u_g)=u_{\delta(g)}$. These maps extend normally by their trace-preserving basis unitaries. Characters are invariant under inner group automorphisms, and $U_\delta T_\psi U_\delta^{-1}=T_{\psi\circ\delta^{-1}}$. Thus $(\chi,[\delta])\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}[T_\chi U_\delta]$ is a well-defined homomorphism for the displayed left action. It is surjective by the untwisted unit-scale case of Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>, whose cochain is then a character.

For injectivity, suppose $T_\chi U_\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(w)$, and write $b=\chi\circ\delta$ and $w=\sum_x a_xu_x$ in $L^2(L(\Gamma))$. The relation $$u_{\delta(g)}wu_g^{-1}=b(g)^{-1}w$$ shows that $|a_x|$ is constant on each orbit of $x\mathrel{%
  \pdfliteral direct{/Span << /ActualText <FEFF21A6> >> BDC}%
  \paperoriginalmapsto
  \pdfliteral direct{EMC}}\delta(g)xg^{-1}$. A nonzero coefficient has a finite orbit by square summability. Its stabilizer $H$ has finite index, and $\delta(h)=xhx^{-1}$ for all $h\in H$. Set $\beta=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(x^{-1})\circ\delta$ and let $N$ be the normal core of $H$. Since $\beta$ fixes $N$ pointwise, $g^{-1}\beta(g)$ centralizes $N$ for every $g\in\Gamma$. The centralizer of a finite-index subgroup is trivial by ICC, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:calculus-germ-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:calculus-germ-test">6</a>; hence $\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(x)$. Now $v=u_x^*w$ implements the pure character twist $b$. Its Fourier-coefficient absolute values are constant on conjugacy classes, so ICC makes $v$ scalar. Therefore $b=1$, whence $\chi=1$, and $[\delta]=1$, proving injectivity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Recovery from the reduced group C\*-algebra

Write $C_r^*(\Gamma)$ for the operator-norm closure of the group algebra in the left regular representation.

<a id="stmt-cor-reduced-cstar"></a>
<div id="cor:reduced-cstar" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22** (Reduced C\*-algebra recovery). *Let $\Gamma\in\mathscr K$ and suppose that $C_r^*(\Gamma)$ has a unique tracial state. If $\Lambda$ is any discrete group and there is a complex-linear $*$-isomorphism $C_r^*(\Gamma)\cong C_r^*(\Lambda)$, then $\Gamma\cong\Lambda$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-reduced-cstar"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-reduced-cstar</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\alpha:C_r^*(\Gamma)\to C_r^*(\Lambda)$ be the given $*$-isomorphism, and denote the canonical traces by $\tau_\Gamma$ and $\tau_\Lambda$. The isomorphism is unital, and uniqueness gives $\tau_\Lambda\circ\alpha=\tau_\Gamma$. Consequently $\alpha$ extends isometrically to a unitary between the trace-GNS Hilbert spaces. This unitary intertwines left multiplication, so conjugation by it extends $\alpha$ to a normal isomorphism $L(\Gamma)\cong L(\Lambda)$.

The source reduced algebra is separable. Distinct canonical group unitaries in the target have trace $2$-norm distance $\sqrt2$, and hence operator-norm distance at least $\sqrt2$; thus $\Lambda$ is countable. Moreover, $L(\Lambda)$ is an infinite-dimensional factor, so $\Lambda$ is infinite and ICC. Indeed, a nonidentity finite conjugacy class would give a nonscalar central class sum. Theorem <a href="#thm:stable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:stable">2</a>, with both cocycles trivial and both amplification scales equal to one, now gives $\Gamma\cong\Lambda$. ◻

</details>

In particular this applies to $\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$: its reduced group C\*-algebra has a unique tracial state by (Bekka 2007, discussion following Corollary 5) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Bekka2007](#ref-Bekka2007)].

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Finite-index endomorphisms of the special linear factor

We state the precise classical fact needed in the final deduction. The finite-index image hypothesis is part of it.

<a id="stmt-lem-sl3-covolume"></a>
<div id="lem:sl3-covolume" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *An injective homomorphism $\alpha:\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)\to\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$ whose image has finite index is surjective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sl3-covolume"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sl3-covolume</summary>

*Proof.* Put $G=\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb R)$ and $\Gamma=\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$. The group $G$ is connected, center-free, simple, of real rank two, and has no compact factor. Both $\Gamma$ and $\alpha(\Gamma)$ are irreducible lattices in $G$. The classical strong-rigidity theorem extends their isomorphism to an analytic automorphism $\Phi$ of $G$; see (Prasad 1973, Theorem B) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Prasad1973](#ref-Prasad1973)]. This form of strong rigidity allows nonuniform lattices and torsion; its excluded real rank-one $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb R)$ case does not occur here. The theorem combines the uniform rigidity of Mostow with the nonuniform higher-rank work of Margulis and Raghunathan and Prasad’s extension, as explained in that reference.

The differential $D=d\Phi_e$ preserves the nondegenerate Killing form $B$ on the Lie algebra: $D^{\mathsf T}BD=B$. Hence $|\det D|=1$. A left-invariant Haar density is therefore preserved at the identity and, by translation, everywhere. Consequently $$\operatorname{covol}(\alpha(\Gamma))
 =\operatorname{covol}(\Phi(\Gamma))
 =\operatorname{covol}(\Gamma).$$ On the other hand the first covolume equals $[\Gamma:\alpha(\Gamma)]\operatorname{covol}(\Gamma)$. The common covolume is positive and finite, so the index equals one. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-sl3-endomorphism"></a>
<div id="cor:sl3-endomorphism" class="corollary">

**Corollary 24**. *Every normal unital complex-linear $*$-endomorphism $\theta$ of $M=L(\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z))$ with $[M:\theta(M)]<\infty$ is surjective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-sl3-endomorphism"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-sl3-endomorphism</summary>

*Proof.* The endomorphism is injective because its kernel is an ultraweakly closed two-sided ideal in a factor, and it preserves the normalized trace. Equip $H=L^2(M)$ with its usual right action and the left action $x\xi=\theta(x)\xi$. It is bifinite, with $$\dim H_M=1,\qquad \dim_M H=[M:\theta(M)].$$ Its actual full-germ decomposition and Equation <a href="#eq:calculus-summand-dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:calculus-summand-dimensions">[eq:calculus-summand-dimensions]</a> show that right dimension one leaves exactly one orbit, a one-dimensional fibre, and $A_d=\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)$. The graph is therefore an isomorphism $\delta:\mathrm{SL}_3(\mathbb Z)\to B_d$, where $B_d$ has finite index in the same group. Lemma <a href="#lem:sl3-covolume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sl3-covolume">23</a> makes $B_d$ the whole group. The left dimension formula now gives $[M:\theta(M)]=1$, so $\theta(M)=M$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Arithmetic exhaustion from the companion supplies the geometric input for the main classification. The model calculus is independent of that input. Actual multiplication and reciprocal dimensions are separate final arguments.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-map",
      "pdf_page": 7,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-map",
          "exact_quote": "Arithmetic exhaustion from the companion supplies the geometric input\nfor the main classification. The model calculus is independent of that input. Actual\nmultiplication and reciprocal dimensions are separate final arguments.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=9mm and 7mm,\n box/.style={draw=linkblue,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,\n text width=43mm,inner sep=6pt,font=\\small},\n arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick,draw=linkblue}]\n\\node[box] (models) {Graph models, full stabilizers,\\\\all maps and fusion};\n\\node[box,right=of models] (geometry) {Companion: geometric\\\\arithmetic exhaustion};\n\\node[box,below=of $(models.south)!0.5!(geometry.south)$,\n text width=57mm] (category) {Twisted group factors, source in $\\mathscr K$:\\\\full-germ fibres and integral dimensions};\n\\node[box,below=14mm of category,xshift=-29mm] (mult) {Bounded products\\\\in germ fibres\\\\and minimal identity corner};\n\\node[box,below=14mm of category,xshift=29mm] (scale) {Reciprocal dimensions\\\\and column equivalences};\n\\node[box,below=of mult] (factor) {Arbitrary factor neighbors\\\\and exact cocycle ratio};\n\\node[box,below=of scale] (maps) {Equal scales and\\\\specified stable maps};\n\\draw[arrow] (models)--(category);\n\\draw[arrow] (geometry)--(category);\n\\draw[arrow] (category)--(mult);\n\\draw[arrow] (category)--(scale);\n\\draw[arrow] (mult)--(factor);\n\\draw[arrow] (scale)--(maps);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flowchart diagram consisting of 7 rectangular boxes arranged in four horizontal tiers, connected by downward directed arrows:\n- Tier 1 (top):\n  - Top-left box: \"Graph models,\\nfull stabilizers,\\nall maps and fusion\"\n  - Top-right box: \"Companion: geometric\\narithmetic exhaustion\"\n  Arrows lead from both boxes down towards the central box in Tier 2.\n- Tier 2 (middle):\n  - Central box: \"Twisted group factors, source in $\\mathcal{K}$:\\nfull-germ fibres and\\nintegral dimensions\"\n  Two downward arrows lead from this central box branching left and right to Tier 3.\n- Tier 3:\n  - Middle-left box: \"Bounded products\\nin germ fibres\\nand minimal\\nidentity corner\"\n  - Middle-right box: \"Reciprocal dimensions\\nand column equivalences\"\n  A downward arrow leads from the middle-left box to the bottom-left box, and a downward arrow leads from the middle-right box to the bottom-right box.\n- Tier 4 (bottom):\n  - Bottom-left box: \"Arbitrary factor neighbors\\nand exact cocycle ratio\"\n  - Bottom-right box: \"Equal scales and\\nspecified stable maps\""
    }
  ],
